32. Once pass or fail stops defining the comparison, the performances show different degrees of accuracy relative to the task.
33. Neither surface sets the ideal: one is rougher and the other smoother through their comparison.
34. Salinity appears as a gradient rather than fresh versus salt.
35. A smooth gradient emerging through dissolved bands.
36. The old sameness no longer governs; their contrast carries the distinction.

### **Structural signatures**

- Differences become available as comparative degrees.
- A privileged standard ceases to dictate the comparison.
- As distinctions borne by the terms' comparative positions.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 1, Cell 08

*Two friends are choosing between two rented bicycles for a weekend trip.*

**Elise:** That red one feels slow. I'll take the blue.

**Marco:** Slow compared with what? I rode the red one around the block and it was fine.

**Elise:** Mine at home, I suppose. It's much lighter.

**Marco:** Neither of these is your bike. Try the blue up the hill before you decide.

**Elise:** All right. The blue climbs more easily, but the handlebars are a stretch.

**Marco:** I had the opposite problem. The red fits me better, but the gears are heavier.

**Elise:** So calling the red one slow isn't quite fair. The blue makes climbing easier, and the red feels steadier on the flat.

**Marco:** And the blue's reach is longer. That's helpful to me, less comfortable for you.

**Elise:** We're treating whichever one we tried first as the normal bike.

**Marco:** Yeah. Let's compare them on the same little route.

*They ride a short loop and return.*

**Elise:** Now I can put it properly: blue is easier uphill, red is easier for me to control on the bends.

**Marco:** Neither is simply the good one and the other the bad one.

**Elise:** For this trip, with that steep lane and those sharp turns, it's a trade-off.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** An initially one-sided judgment becomes a determinate comparison of differing advantages and limitations, without either bicycle defining normality for the other.
- **Sufficiency:** Both bicycles acquire intelligible standings through comparison on the same route, rather than one being judged as a departure from a privileged standard.
- **Persistence:** The distinctions remain constituted through the shared comparative bearings—climbing effort, reach, and handling—without installing either bicycle as the norm.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 02, *Binary bearing*. There, one focal item stands as an exception relative to a governing expectation. Here neither item has the authority to define the other as exceptional; the differences are reciprocal and graded.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 2, Cell 08

*Two committee members are comparing the hall's two entrances.*

**Conor:** People keep saying the front entrance is the proper one and the back is the poor substitute.

**Meena:** That's how we've always described them. But the front steps are awkward for a wheelchair.

**Conor:** The rear entrance has a ramp, though it's farther from the bus stop.

**Meena:** And the front door is wider for moving furniture.

**Conor:** So neither entrance is simply better.

**Meena:** For step-free access the rear is easier. For bringing in the stage flats, the front is easier.

**Conor:** What about people arriving after dark?

**Meena:** The front has better lighting at present. We're fixing the rear light next week.

**Conor:** Then let's stop calling one “main” and the other “inferior” in the visitor notes.

**Meena:** We can describe the actual differences. Which is easier depends on what someone needs to do.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** The entrances become intelligibly differentiated without the “proper entrance” setting the standard for all comparisons.
- **Sufficiency:** Each entrance has relative advantages and limitations through comparable needs; neither acts as the imposed norm.
- **Persistence:** Their distinction obtains through these context-sensitive comparisons, not an inherited ranking.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 05, *Context-bound mark*. Both are comparison-dependent, but here a prior normative ordering must lose its authority before the non-hierarchical contrasts can stand.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 3, Cell 08

*Two people are choosing rooms for a community music programme.*

**Grace:** Everyone says the upstairs room is the good one and the basement is the poor one. Should we put the singing class upstairs?

**Noel:** Maybe. The upstairs room has better daylight, but it's quite echoey.

**Grace:** The basement's duller, though.

**Noel:** Listen to the piano down there. The notes are much clearer than they are upstairs.

**Grace:** True. But the basement has those low beams. Would it suit the movement workshop?

**Noel:** Probably not. The upstairs room has more clear floor space.

**Grace:** What about the small room off the foyer?

**Noel:** Quiet enough for one-to-one lessons, but too cramped for a group.

**Grace:** So “good room” isn't a useful ranking. For singing, the basement might be better acoustically; for movement, upstairs is better; for individual lessons, the foyer room works.

**Noel:** We should also check step-free access before assigning anything.

**Grace:** Yes. Let's write down what each room offers rather than rank them from best to worst.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** A fixed good-room/bad-room ranking loses its authority, and the rooms acquire different relative advantages depending on the activity.
- **Sufficiency:** The participants compare acoustics, space and scale across several rooms, without allowing the old ranking to decide every case. The access check remains outstanding, so no final programme allocation is claimed.
- **Persistence:** The distinctions stand as need-relative bearings that will inform later assignments; none of the rooms is simply “the good one” for all purposes.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 07, *Opposed standing*. That form would suffice if the rooms merely recovered independent descriptions after the old ranking was dropped. Here the accomplishment is the comparative pattern itself: each room's advantage changes with what it is compared for.
- **Decisive evidence:** “For singing, the basement might be better acoustically; for movement, upstairs is better; for individual lessons, the foyer room works.”

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

## **Cell 09 — Exclusive cut**

**Canonical identity:** Differentiation × Release × Efficacy

**Coordinate-generated form:** Released exclusionary efficacy

**Original X–Y traversal:** X selects only one among Y options.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** An inherited conflation loses authority over response, allowing relevant differences to govern selection or exclusion. The completion is discrimination freed from a classification that previously overrode it.

**Canonical short form:** Not admitted.

### **Collected sentences and examples**

1. Once the blanket label of 'safe' was discarded, the inspector rejected the cracked beams and approved the sound ones.
2. Once the label 'broken' was set aside, the mechanic salvaged the working parts and discarded only the damaged ones.
3. The doctor stopped treating every red mark alike and isolated the ones needing attention.
4. When the school abandoned its single 'late' category, it admitted delayed buses but penalised deliberate absences.
5. I no longer throw clothes out just because they're faded; only the ones that have become unwearable go.
6. She stopped accepting every invitation out of politeness and began saying yes only to the occasions she genuinely wanted to attend.
7. Being old is no longer a reason to discard a tool; we keep the ones that still work.
8. We stopped rejecting books for their untidy covers and began excluding only those with missing text.
9. Instead of refusing every unsolicited email, I now keep genuine enquiries and delete the advertising.
10. We no longer exclude all second-hand toys; only those that fail the safety check are refused.
11. **Nature:** Floodwaters breach a levee and select new channels by topography
12. **Mathematics:** A classifier excludes a case based on an operative property rather than an obsolete category.
13. **Music:** An adaptive accompaniment rejects notes that clash with the performed chord after its old fixed key filter stops deciding acceptability.
14. **Visual art:** An installation stops sorting marks by artist label and instead projects them by measured hue.
15. **Narrative:** A protagonist abandons inherited status labels and chooses allies from their actual actions.
16. **Architecture:** A new safety scheme routes occupants by measured risk rather than legacy room labels.
17. **Social:** Decisions cease following status labels and respond to demonstrated needs.
18. **Perception:** A learned category ceases governing action and actual shapes determine grasp choice.
19. **Computation:** A rules engine replaces inherited labels with actual properties for admission decisions.
20. At a gate, a valid membership check excludes entries that an old catch-all category would have admitted.
21. Floodwater escapes a breached levee.
22. Terrain now directs flow beyond the former confinement.
23. A panel abandons school prestige as its filter and excludes an applicant who lacks a necessary safety qualification.
24. Obsidian gate with a single luminous criterion cut through it: the old stamp is erased, the new cut works.
25. The gate is changed when relevance, not inheritance, decides.
26. The sorting machine used to send every red object left and every blue object right. That rule kept misdirecting the parts. The colour gate is removed; sensors now send each piece according to its actual size. The old classification no longer gets to decide what happens.
27. An editor stops accepting every sentence labelled “technical” and retains only those that explain the mechanism accurately.
28. An adjustable sorter ceases routing objects by colour and separates them by size; the former colour grouping no longer governs the outlets.
29. A support service stops assigning identical assistance by diagnosis alone and responds to the clients’ distinct practical needs.
30. The sorter now rejects crumpled tokens; the label 'token' no longer guarantees admission.
31. The packing routine excludes fragile pieces from the heavy-load box, regardless of their former 'small item' classification.
32. A revised eligibility rule rejects one application.
33. An old boundary dissolves while a new gate selects flow.
34. Now that the old label has lost its hold, the difference can guide action.

### **Structural signatures**

- A previously suppressed distinction governs selection.
- An inherited conflation loses its authority over the decision.
- As a usable power to select or exclude accordingly.

### **Differentiation — Documentary register**

| Cell | Canonical name     | Numbered examples collected |
| ---- | ------------------ | --------------------------- |
| 01   | Focal mark         | 38                          |
| 02   | Binary bearing     | 38                          |
| 03   | Boundary act       | 42                          |
| 04   | Scope standing     | 38                          |
| 05   | Context-bound mark | 48                          |

|      | **Total**          | **346**                     |

Additionally preserved: 27 structural signature expressions and one complete extended dialogue.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 1, Cell 09

*Two librarians are deciding which donated puzzles can go into the children's lending collection.*

**Tessa:** The labels on these boxes say ages five to eight. We could put that whole stack on the shelf.

**Colm:** This one has loose magnets small enough to swallow.

**Tessa:** But it's marked five-plus.

**Colm:** The age label isn't telling us whether those pieces are secure. Look: two have already come off.

**Tessa:** And this one says eight-plus, but it's just a large wooden maze. Nothing comes loose.

**Colm:** So we shouldn't decide by the printed ages alone.

**Tessa:** What should decide whether something goes out?

**Colm:** Whether all the pieces are present, intact, and safe for the borrowers it's intended for.

**Tessa:** Let's check them individually. The wooden maze is complete. The magnetic one isn't safe in its present state.

**Colm:** Here's another five-plus box with a snapped plastic edge.

**Tessa:** That stays out too. This eight-plus jigsaw is missing four pieces; not dangerous, but not ready to lend.

**Colm:** And this six-plus matching game is complete and sound.

**Tessa:** So the age stickers can still help families choose, but they don't decide admission to our shelf.

**Colm:** Exactly. The matching game and maze go in. The broken, unsafe and incomplete sets don't.

**Tessa:** I'll make a separate repair box for the ones we're excluding.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** Relevant differences in condition and safety take authority over lending decisions previously governed by the manufacturer's age categories.
- **Sufficiency:** The inherited labels no longer determine admission; the actual distinctions between sound, unsafe, broken and incomplete puzzles control the decision in each case.
- **Persistence:** The completed distinction is operative as an exclusionary admission rule: puzzles that fail the relevant checks do not enter the lending collection regardless of age label.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 07, *Opposed standing*. Both displace an inherited categorization, but Cell 07 establishes what differences stand; here the distinctions themselves determine what is admitted and what is refused.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 2, Cell 09

*Two people are reviewing donated electrical items for the hall.*

**Mick:** This box says “working equipment.” Can we put it all into the storeroom?

**Tara:** Who wrote that?

**Mick:** The previous caretaker, years ago.

**Tara:** Let's check what's here. This extension lead has a cracked casing.

**Mick:** The old label says it worked when it was packed.

**Tara:** That doesn't make it safe now. And this lamp's plug has been replaced but hasn't been tested.

**Mick:** The portable radio has a current test label, though.

**Tara:** Yes. Let's use the current inspection records, not that old box label.

**Mick:** So the radio can join the approved equipment. The lead is out, and the lamp waits for testing.

**Tara:** Correct. Neither untested nor damaged items go into public use.

**Mick:** I'll mark the shelving so nobody mistakes the quarantine box for approved stock.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** Distinctions grounded in present electrical condition displace an inherited “working” label and acquire authority over admission to use.
- **Sufficiency:** The former category no longer decides; documented condition and testing determine inclusion, exclusion, or quarantine.
- **Persistence:** These discriminations operate as the rule governing which items may enter public service.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 07, *Opposed standing*. Cell 07 establishes distinctions free of the old label; here they directly govern admission and refusal.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Dialogue archive — Cycle 3, Cell 09

*Two volunteers are deciding which donated plants can go into a school garden.*

**Rosa:** The box says “approved seedlings.” Can I put all of these on the planting trolley?

**Dylan:** That label's from the spring sale. We still need to check them.

**Rosa:** The tomatoes look healthy. These basil plants have spotted leaves, though.

**Dylan:** Could be harmless damage. Let's ask the gardening teacher what she wants us to check.

**Rosa:** She's left the intake sheet here: roots sound, no visible pest infestation, no suspect leaf disease.

**Dylan:** Then we can't use the old “approved” sticker as our test.

**Rosa:** Look under this basil leaf. There are insects clustered along the stem.

**Dylan:** That tray should stay away from the garden. Put it in the inspection area.

**Rosa:** The tomatoes have clean leaves and firm roots. They can go on the planting trolley.

**Dylan:** This mint has no pests, but its roots are badly rotted.

**Rosa:** Inspection area too, then. We shouldn't plant it just because the box says approved.

**Dylan:** I'll cross out the old label and mark the trays individually.

**Rosa:** Good. Only the ones that pass today's checks go into the beds.

#### Structural check

- **Change:** The inherited blanket approval is displaced by present-condition distinctions that determine which plants may be planted and which must be withheld.
- **Sufficiency:** Inspection criteria actually govern the tomatoes' admission and the basil and mint trays' exclusion from planting. The difference is not merely recorded; it changes what happens to the plants.
- **Persistence:** The planting trolley and inspection area embody a continuing separation under the current intake rule, independent of the old box label.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 07, *Opposed standing*. That cell would be achieved by recognising the plants' different conditions without settling their handling. Here those distinctions have operative force in allowing or preventing planting.
- **Decisive evidence:** “Only the ones that pass today's checks go into the beds,” after the participants have separated the inspected trays accordingly.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

*End of the first documentary instalment. Additional archive dialogues and remaining source passages for Cells 01–09 are not yet incorporated. [Historical instalment note; the three-cycle dialogue archive has since been fully incorporated.] The next family in the canonical sequence is Opening, Cells 10–18.*

# **II. Opening — Cells 10–18**

## **Cell 10 — Source field**

**Canonical identity:** Opening × Focal × Standing

**Coordinate-generated form:** Focal available field

**Original X–Y traversal:** X expands outward into Y.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** A definite avenue of access becomes available at the point that governs entry. The achieved opening is an established entrance into what had been inaccessible.

**Canonical short form:** From here.

### **Collected sentences and examples**

1. Behind the ivy-covered wall, they discovered the entrance to a forgotten garden.

2. A small doorway behind the tapestry gave access to the sealed chamber.

3. The archaeologists found the mouth of a tunnel beneath the altar.

4. Under the snow, a stairway descended to the station no one could enter.

5. There's a bursary for people who want to return to education later in life.

6. If you want to hear her side of the story, her diary is a place to begin.

7. I've found a way into the poem through its opening image.

8. There is now a counselling service where bereaved parents can begin seeking support.

9. I've found an introductory course for anyone who wants to understand astronomy.

10. There is a small hatch beneath the stairs leading into the previously inaccessible cellar.

11. **Nature:** A seed coat cracks at the radicle emergence site

12. **Mathematics:** A newly defined domain admits a previously undefined input to a function.

13. **Music:** A brief pickup establishes a recognisable entrance into an otherwise continuous instrumental texture.

14. **Visual art:** A dark aperture opens a definite line of sight through an installation.

15. **Narrative:** A letter establishes the entry point into an otherwise opaque mystery.

16. **Architecture:** A single opening creates a definite entrance to a walled court.

17. **Social:** An introduction opens a definite avenue to a new group.

18. **Perception:** An occluder moves and exposes one definite sightline.

19. **Computation:** A previously inaccessible endpoint gets an addressable interface.

20. The first page of a book asks readers to enter an unfamiliar order.

21. A previously unknown doorway is found. Access is now available at that doorway.

22. A seed coat opens at the emerging root.

23. The developing root gains a definite passage outward.

24. A local library opens a staffed digital-help desk for the first time.

25. A luminous brass keyhole in a deep blue wall: one focal opening, not an entire route.

26. Possibility begins at a real entrance.

27. A household discovers an accessible entrance to a garden that had seemed enclosed on every side. A fracture opens a vent through an otherwise enclosing rock layer. The vent is an avenue of access independently of anyone finding it.

28. A previously opaque poem acquires a definite way in when its opening image becomes intelligible as the organising invitation.

29. A fracture opens a vent through an otherwise enclosing rock layer. The vent is an avenue of access independently of anyone finding it.

30. A household discovers an accessible entrance to a garden that had seemed enclosed on every side.

31. The new gate gives the enclosed garden a definite way in.

32. The guide establishes the first folder as an entry into the otherwise unapproachable archive.

33. An entrance to a sealed courtyard is found.

34. One illuminated doorway in a closed wall.

35. This opening is enough to reach what was closed off before.

36. A doorway is established as an available entrance.

37. A seed coat cracks at the radicle emergence site.

38. A new volcanic vent connecting magma to the surface (observed).

### **Structural signatures**

- A definite avenue of access becomes available.
- Entry is accomplished at one governing point.
- As an established opening at that point.

### **Extended documentary example — Natural World — seed coat opening**

**Primary illustration:** A seed coat cracks at the radicle emergence site. **Type:** Observed; attribution remains a structural research hypothesis.

**Candidates considered:** radicle emergence in a germinating seed; a geyser vent opens; a sea cave entrance formed by erosion . **Selection:** The primary was chosen because its governing physical distinction can be watched or measured, while the alternatives expose different ways the same purported completion could arise.

**Story.** After imbibition and germination signalling, embryonic root growth and weakened covering tissues produce a rupture through which the radicle emerges. Not every crack marks germination: the meaningful opening is at the site enabling an organised root exit. Subsequent root elongation can be studied separately.

**Visual encounter:** Macro time-lapse of germination, paused at first radicle breach with no further growth shown. The website should first show the transition silently, ask what changes and what remains, then disclose the canonical name. **Observable criterion:** Imaging confirms emergence of radicle through the seed coat or endosperm at a localized rupture.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 1**

*Two visitors have arrived at a small art centre after walking around the building looking for an entrance.*

**June:** The front doors are locked. Are we too late?

**Alex:** The exhibition's supposed to be open until six. It's only four.

**June:** Maybe we're at the wrong building.

**Alex:** The poster in the window has the exhibition title. This is definitely it.

**June:** What about that gate? It looks like it leads to a courtyard.

**Alex:** There's a sign, but I can't read it from here.

**June:** It says, 'Gallery entrance through courtyard.'

**Alex:** So the front doors aren't the public entrance.

**June:** The gate's open. Shall we check?

**Alex:** There's a reception desk just inside that glass door. I can see people going in.

**June:** Good. I thought the whole place was closed.

**Alex:** No, we've found the entrance. It's through here.

**June:** I'll message Sam, then. He's meeting us and he'll probably try the locked doors too.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 1

- **Change:** An apparently inaccessible art centre acquires a definite public point of entry.
- **Sufficiency:** The signed courtyard entrance is identified as the locus through which the exhibition becomes available. The achievement is finding the established avenue of access, not demonstrating a new ability to force a blocked passage.
- **Persistence:** The entrance stands as an available point of access for the visitors and for someone arriving later.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 12, *Branching output*. That cell requires an operative capacity to get through a decisive bottleneck; here an entrance is identified and established as available, without altering the bottleneck's traversability.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 2**

*Two new volunteers have arrived to help paint the hall.*

**Amina:** Every outside door seems locked. Did we come on the wrong day?

**Pádraig:** The sign says volunteer work is on today. Maybe they're all upstairs.

**Amina:** There's a small arrow by the side passage. “Volunteer entrance.”

**Pádraig:** I thought that went to the boiler room.

**Amina:** Look, the door at the end has a welcome sign and a table of name badges inside.

**Pádraig:** It's open. That's where everyone checks in.

**Amina:** So we don't have to ring the main door?

**Pádraig:** No. The volunteer entrance is through this passage.

**Amina:** I'll message Noor. She's coming later and might walk around the building like we did.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 2

- **Change:** A seemingly inaccessible volunteer session acquires a determinate point of entry.
- **Sufficiency:** The marked open side door is established as the relevant avenue of admission at the hall's boundary.
- **Persistence:** It stands as a known entrance available to subsequent volunteers.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 12, *Branching output*. Here an established entrance is found, not a bottleneck transformed into executable passage.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 3**

*Two walkers arrive at a nature reserve where the old main gate is shut.*

**Jules:** The map shows a path into the reserve here, but the gate's chained.

**Owen:** There's a small sign saying “Visitor access moved.” The arrow points towards the car park.

**Jules:** Maybe that's just where they want people to park.

**Owen:** Let's see. There's another sign at the far end: “Reserve entrance — footpath.”

**Jules:** And a gate in the fence. Is it locked too?

**Owen:** No, it has a pedestrian latch. There's a board inside with the trail map.

**Jules:** So this is the entrance now, not the chained gate by the road.

**Owen:** Looks like it. We can tell the others where to come.

**Jules:** Wait, the board says the marsh trail is closed for repairs.

**Owen:** That's all right. We were only trying to find the entrance. We'll choose a walk once everyone's here.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 3

- **Change:** The reserve, initially inaccessible at the familiar gate, acquires a determinate visitor point of entry.
- **Sufficiency:** The official sign, pedestrian gate and trail board establish this particular entrance as available; the participants do not need to complete a trail to settle where entry is provided.
- **Persistence:** The entrance remains identifiable for the rest of their group even though a separate route within the reserve is closed.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 12, *Branching output*. The dialogue stops before overcoming a route bottleneck and demonstrating several executable onward paths. The achievement is the standing availability of one entry point.
- **Decisive evidence:** “So this is the entrance now, not the chained gate by the road,” followed by the explicit deferral of choosing a walk.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

## Cell 11 — Source-carried divergence

**Canonical identity:** Opening × Focal × Bearing

**Coordinate-generated form:** Source-bearing divergence

**Original X–Y traversal:** X moves away from source Y along shifting paths.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** Access becomes definite through an indispensable enabling connection at its point of entry. The opening remains constituted by that connection, as admission through a key, source, support, or qualifying link.

**Canonical short form:** From here, either way.

### **Collected sentences and examples**

1. One brass key opened the passage from which every room in the old tower could be reached.

2. Her membership card admitted her to the archive and all its reading rooms.

3. The password unlocked a portal through which dozens of services could be reached.

4. The old lift was the only connection to the observatory at the summit.

5. Through my union membership, I have access to independent legal advice.

6. Knowing Irish gives her direct access to stories that have never been translated.

7. As a registered foster carer, she has access to the support services available through the agency.

8. Her father's consent gives her access to the medical records he wants her to read.

9. Through the sign-language interpreter, the visitor has access to the speaker's account.

10. The microscope gives us access to details of the fabric that are invisible to the naked eye.

11. **Nature:** A virus binds the requisite cellular receptor for entry competence

12. **Mathematics:** An inverse function exists locally because the derivative is nonzero under the theorem's hypotheses.

13. **Music:** A singer's entry becomes available through a stable pitch supplied by an accompanying drone.

14. **Visual art:** A viewing slit admits the scene only from a specified sightline.

15. **Narrative:** A hidden passage is accessible only through the knowledge in one inherited diary.

16. **Architecture:** A ramp gives admission through the entrance under its necessary slope condition.

17. **Social:** An invitation enables entry through the inviter's standing.

18. **Perception:** A cue opens access to a target through a necessary attentional set.

19. **Computation:** An authentication token enables access through a required credential link.

20. A library becomes accessible through one qualifying membership, which still determines that access.

21. A virus engages its host-cell receptor.

22. A particular molecular match establishes entry competence.

23. A trainee receives workshop access through a sponsor's required accreditation.

24. Copper tether anchored to a turquoise threshold: access and source are inseparable.

25. Follow the opening to the link that makes it possible.

26. A key gives a visitor access through a locked entrance while its correspondence with the lock constitutes that access. A gated pore becomes accessible to an ion under the required gating condition; availability is constituted by that physical condition, not by recognition.

27. An allusion opens an unfamiliar passage to a reader through a recognised source; that source constitutes the particular access achieved.

28. A gated pore becomes accessible to an ion under the required gating condition; availability is constituted by that physical condition, not by recognition.

29. A key gives a visitor access through a locked entrance while its correspondence with the lock constitutes that access.

30. Through this common introduction, either of the two groups is now accessible to us.

31. The shared stem gives both branches a connection to the same water supply.

32. A bridgehead gives access to either shore route.

33. One junction holding two suspended routes.

34. This access exists by way of that connection.

35. A visitor’s access through that entrance is established through a valid key.

36. A virus binds the requisite cellular receptor for entry competence.

37. Compatible pollen–stigma molecular recognition enabling pollen-tube entry (observed).

### **Structural signatures**

- An avenue of access becomes available through an enabling link.
- A focal connection constitutes admission.
- As access that depends upon that enabling connection.

### **Extended documentary example — Natural World — receptor-dependent entry**

**Primary illustration:** A virus binds the requisite cellular receptor for entry competence. **Type:** Observed; receptor-dependence species-specific; attribution remains a structural research hypothesis.

**Candidates considered:** virus binding to cellular receptor; pollen tube acceptance at stigma; ectomycorrhizal root recognition . **Selection:** The primary was chosen because its governing physical distinction can be watched or measured, while the alternatives expose different ways the same purported completion could arise.

**Story.** For viruses that require a particular receptor, attachment establishes a specific route into a host cell. Receptor engagement is not identical to completed internalisation: coreceptors, proteases or membrane conditions may still be needed. The completion proposed here is receptor-dependent eligibility for an entry pathway, verified independently of downstream productive infection.

**Three governing questions — one completed reality.** **What changed?** A previously inaccessible membrane acquires a permitted molecular interaction for a particular virion. **What made it sufficient?** Receptor binding plus necessary cofactors yields demonstrable entry competence under defined conditions, not mere nonspecific adsorption. **How does it remain?** Continued molecular engagement and permissive membrane conditions sustain the entry pathway until fusion or uptake. These do not mark three independent stages: all identify the same candidate event under complementary physical tests.

**Visual encounter:** Single-particle fluorescence imaging of virions on cells with and without receptor; reveal receptor only after comparison. The website should first show the transition silently, ask what changes and what remains, then disclose the canonical name. **Observable criterion:** Receptor binding plus necessary cofactors yields demonstrable entry competence under defined conditions, not mere nonspecific adsorption.

**Alternative from a different domain:** Compatible pollen–stigma molecular recognition enabling pollen-tube entry (observed).

**Discipline / possible failure:** Some viruses use multiple receptors or alternative pathways; no universal receptor rule exists.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 1**

*Two neighbours are at the entrance to a local history festival, looking at their booking confirmation.*

**Aisling:** I thought we had to choose between the house tour and the garden talk when we booked.

**Ben:** So did I. The email only gives us one ticket.

**Aisling:** It says “festival admission” at the top. Maybe that's just for the grounds.

**Ben:** The house tour starts here, but the garden talk is through that arch. Are they checking separate tickets?

**Aisling:** Let's ask before we queue. Look, there's a list of what's included on the back of the confirmation.

**Ben:** “House tour or garden talk, subject to space.” I missed that.

**Aisling:** But does the same ticket cover whichever one we choose?

**Ben:** There's a note underneath: “Present your festival admission at either entrance.”

**Aisling:** So we don't have to pick now?

**Ben:** Apparently not. We can use this booking for either event.

**Aisling:** I fancy the garden talk if the weather holds.

**Ben:** And the house if it rains.

**Aisling:** Good. I'll keep the confirmation handy. That's what gets us into whichever one we go to.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 1

- **Change:** Two apparently separate admission routes become accessible as genuine alternatives through a single confirmed entitlement.
- **Sufficiency:** The festival admission is the decisive enabling source; its validity constitutes access to either event, rather than simply placing two activities on a list of possibilities.
- **Persistence:** Either option remains open through the continuing validity of the same booking, which must be presented at the chosen entrance.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. Cell 13 establishes a range of available routes across a distributed field; here the divergence remains constituted by one focal source of admission that authorizes either choice.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 2**

*Two residents are examining their new hall membership cards.*

**Ellen:** Do I need a separate pass for the reading room and the craft workshop?

**Bilal:** I thought we did. The rooms have separate doors.

**Ellen:** The membership leaflet says either room is included on weekday afternoons.

**Bilal:** But only with a current hall card, right?

**Ellen:** Yes. It says to show the card at whichever room you're using.

**Bilal:** Let me check the expiry date. Mine's valid until next spring.

**Ellen:** So our cards give us access to either one, though we still have to reserve a craft table if it's busy.

**Bilal:** That's helpful. I can decide when I arrive whether to read or join the workshop.

**Ellen:** As long as the card's current.

**Bilal:** I'll keep mine in my wallet, then.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 2

- **Change:** Two apparently separate facilities become alternative admissions through one valid membership.
- **Sufficiency:** The focal membership entitlement is the constitutive source of access to either room, rather than merely one of several possible routes.
- **Persistence:** The alternatives remain open through the continuing validity of the card and its admission terms.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. Cell 13 establishes multiple routes across a field; here a single enabling source constitutes either opening.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### **Extended documentary example — Ordinary dialogue, Cycle 3**

*Two volunteers are helping a new member understand a community workshop's access card.*

**Mabel:** The ceramics room and the sewing room both have card readers. Do I need to register separately for each?

**Tariq:** I thought so. They used to have separate sign-up sheets.

**Mabel:** The welcome email says my membership covers the shared workshop spaces, but it doesn't name the doors.

**Tariq:** Let's check the membership guide. Here: “A current general membership card permits entry to either studio during staffed hours.” The kiln room has a separate induction.

**Mabel:** So the same card covers ceramics and sewing, but not the kiln room?

**Tariq:** That's how I read it. Let's ask at the desk before we promise anything.

**Mabel:** The desk volunteer says the readers use the same membership record. She can see both studios enabled on my card.

**Tariq:** Then the two studio options come with that one membership, not two independent registrations.

**Mabel:** I can decide which one to use when I come on Thursday.

**Tariq:** Yes, while your membership's current and staff are here.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 3

- **Change:** Two apparently separate studio admissions become recognised as alternatives carried by one membership entitlement.
- **Sufficiency:** The guide and membership record establish that the same qualifying card constitutes access to either studio. The kiln room's separate induction also clarifies the entitlement's limit.
- **Persistence:** The two openings remain available through the card's continuing validity and staffed-hours condition.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. Two accessible rooms alone could amount to a distributed set of options. Here their common availability specifically depends on one focal membership source.
- **Decisive evidence:** “The readers use the same membership record. She can see both studios enabled on my card.”

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

**Cell 11 documentary count:** 37 numbered examples; 3 structural signatures; 4 extended demonstrations.

## Cell 12 — Branching output

**Canonical identity:** Opening × Focal × Efficacy

**Coordinate-generated form:** Focal generative opening

**Original X–Y traversal:** X branches into multiple Y’s.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** A decisive bottleneck becomes passable in practice. The opening is accomplished as the capacity to get through where continuation previously failed.

**Canonical short form:** More ways open.

### **Collected sentences and examples**

1. When the jammed gate finally lifted, the ambulance could drive through.

2. Once the fallen tree was cleared, the supply truck could reach the village.

3. The locksmith freed the stuck latch and the trapped family escaped.

4. Removing a single stone let the floodwater run into the drainage channel.

5. Once she learned to read music, she could begin exploring pieces nobody had taught her.

6. Now that we've found a venue, we can start arranging the speakers, exhibitions and workshops.

7. Finding someone to mind the children meant I could finally attend the interview.

8. As soon as the funding came through, the school could begin buying the instruments.

9. Once the proof of identity arrived, I could submit the application.

10. When the missing ingredient turned up, we could finally start making the cake.

11. **Nature:** An axillary plant meristem begins producing a new shoot branch

12. **Mathematics:** A narrow feasible bottleneck becomes traversable when its constraint is relaxed.

13. **Music:** A player learns the crossing technique needed to execute a previously blocked rapid shift on a violin.

14. **Visual art:** Changing a single occluding plane makes light pass through an otherwise blocked opening.

15. **Narrative:** A single coded phrase opens a practical route through a sealed archive.

16. **Architecture:** A bottleneck is made traversable by the users it had excluded.

17. **Social:** A previously excluded guest becomes able to join a gathering.

18. **Perception:** A narrow visual bottleneck becomes traversable by pursuit.

19. **Computation:** A fixed gateway begins passing requests successfully.

20. Every map leaves something out so that something else becomes easier to find.

21. Every public library offers strangers access to work completed by absent minds.

22. A telescope extends sight without removing the need to interpret what appears.

23. At low tide, hidden rock pools briefly become neighborhoods open to inspection.

24. The newspaper headline compresses complicated events into a doorway for public attention.

25. The harbor fog erases landmarks, forcing sailors to trust other forms of guidance.

26. Many bacteria reproduce by dividing one cell into two daughter cells.

27. A jammed exit is made usable. People can now pass through it into several directions.

28. A dormant plant bud begins a new branch.

29. A single growth point generates new continuation.

30. A reliable ramp is installed and tested; wheelchair users can now enter the rehearsal room.

31. Bright apricot current visibly passing a narrow black aperture: flow proves the opening.

32. The opening counts when something gets through.

33. A lowered threshold makes a doorway usable by a wheelchair rather than merely marking where entry ought to occur. A channel widens enough for a previously trapped particle to pass. The new capacity exists without an observer.

34. A conversational reformulation lets someone answer a question they previously could not enter into at all.

35. A channel widens enough for a previously trapped particle to pass. The new capacity exists without an observer.

36. A lowered threshold makes a doorway usable by a wheelchair rather than merely marking where entry ought to occur.

37. The newly opened junction lets the walker continue either along the bank or into the woods.

38. From this starting move, the game now supports three playable continuations.

39. A bud begins producing multiple shoots.

40. One stem flowering into three active branches.

41. This access turns a dead end into a route.

42. A wheelchair can now pass through the entrance.

43. Basaltic magma feeding a newly branching fissure eruption (observed in some eruptions)

### Structural signatures

- A blocked continuation becomes practicable.
- A decisive bottleneck is made passable.
- As the effective capacity to proceed through it.

### Extended demonstration — One Atlas

**Primary illustration:** An axillary plant meristem begins producing a new shoot branch. **Type:** Observed; attribution remains a structural research hypothesis.

**Candidates considered:** axillary bud activation; groundwater spring generating channels; fungal hyphal branching . **Selection:** The primary was chosen because its governing physical distinction can be watched or measured, while the alternatives expose different ways the same purported completion could arise.

**Story.** In a plant, a previously dormant axillary meristem becomes active under hormonal and resource conditions. It generates a shoot that can itself produce leaves and later branches. The result is not the existence of a bud: it is its acquired, local generative capacity. Bud activation can be reversible, and branch pattern depends on species and environment.

**Three governing questions — one completed reality.** **What changed?** A focal dormant meristem becomes a productive origin of growth. **What made it sufficient?** Sustained cell divisions and emergence of a new shoot axis demonstrate generation of a new pathway. **How does it remain?** Meristem maintenance and vascular supply sustain further extension while conditions are adequate. These do not mark three independent stages: all identify the same candidate event under complementary physical tests.

**Structural identity and nearest contrast.** Cell 10: an available exit; Cell 12: an active origin making additional continuations. The classification concerns the specified change and its sufficiency, not the object simply having three separable attributes.

**Visual encounter:** Time-lapse of one node developing a lateral shoot, with vascular tracer marking new supply. The website should first show the transition silently, ask what changes and what remains, then disclose the canonical name. **Observable criterion:** Sustained cell divisions and emergence of a new shoot axis demonstrate generation of a new pathway.

**Alternative from a different domain:** Basaltic magma feeding a newly branching fissure eruption (observed in some eruptions).

**Discipline / possible failure:** The number of growth directions should not be equated with limitless options.

### Extended dialogue — Cycle 1

*Two staff members are setting up a pop-up market in an old courtyard. Their trolley is outside a narrow service gate.*

**Dara:** The stalls are in there, but this trolley won't fit through the pedestrian turnstile.

**Mina:** Isn't there a wide gate beside it?

**Dara:** There is, but I couldn't get it open. I thought it was bolted from the other side.

**Mina:** The sign says to lift the handle before pulling. Did you try that?

**Dara:** I pulled it straight towards me. Hang on.

**Mina:** Lift first. There, it's moving.

**Dara:** That's a relief. Shall we try the trolley?

**Mina:** I'll steer. You hold the gate.

**Dara:** Through! There's plenty of room.

**Mina:** And we can get to the tables on the left from here.

**Dara:** The loading area is on the right, isn't it? We need to take the boxes there first.

**Mina:** Yes. Both paths start inside this gate.

**Dara:** So once the gate's open we can bring equipment to either side, instead of carrying everything piece by piece through the turnstile.

**Mina:** Exactly. Let's get the heavier boxes in while we've got the trolley.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 1

- **Change:** A decisive entrance changes from an apparent obstruction into a usable passage that enables several continuations of the setup.
- **Sufficiency:** Successfully opening the wide gate and moving the loaded trolley through it establish the operative capacity at the bottleneck. From that access point, distinct practical routes into the courtyard become executable.
- **Persistence:** The completion is the acquired ability to bring equipment through the gate and direct it along either internal route, not merely knowledge that an entrance exists.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 11, *Source-carried divergence*. In Cell 11 options obtain through a valid source of entitlement. Here the point of opening is an accomplished practical capacity at a bottleneck, generating different possible continuations.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended dialogue — Cycle 2

*Two volunteers are trying to move equipment through an interior doorway.*

**Jin:** The folding tables won't turn through this doorway with the door only opening halfway.

**Molly:** There's a wedge behind it. Is that what's stopping it?

**Jin:** No, the overhead closer catches. Watch.

**Molly:** There's a little hold-open catch near the top. Maybe that lets it swing farther.

**Jin:** Try lifting it while I push.

**Molly:** There! The door's fully back against the wall.

**Jin:** Let's test the table. Yes, it comes through without scraping.

**Molly:** And now we can turn left towards the supper room or right towards the stage.

**Jin:** Before, we couldn't get these tables through at all.

**Molly:** I'll show the setup team how to open the catch properly.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 2

- **Change:** A critical doorway becomes traversable for equipment, enabling distinct continuations to two working areas.
- **Sufficiency:** The successful loaded passage demonstrates operative access through the bottleneck, not merely recognition of the doorway.
- **Persistence:** The hall team acquires a repeatable capacity to carry tables through that point and onward to either area.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 10, *Source field*. Cell 10 establishes where entry is available; here completion is the effective overcoming of a passage constraint.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended dialogue — Cycle 3

*Two stagehands are trying to move scenery through a theatre's narrow workshop exit.*

**Rina:** The flat won't pass through with that door stopping halfway. We'll have to take the frame apart.

**Sol:** Before we do, is the door meant to open farther? There's a bolt near the top.

**Rina:** That might hold the door against the wall. I can't reach it while I'm holding the flat.

**Sol:** Put the scenery down. I'll support the door while you release the catch.

**Rina:** There. It's swung right back. Let's try the frame.

**Sol:** Straight through. I thought the width of the opening was the problem.

**Rina:** It was the door leaf taking up the space. Now the flat's through, where does it go?

**Sol:** This one goes to the rehearsal stage. The painted backdrop behind it goes to the loading bay.

**Rina:** Can both turn from here?

**Sol:** Yes. I'll take the flat left. You can wheel the backdrop right; the corridor's clear.

**Rina:** Good. We can move both without dismantling them.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 3

- **Change:** An obstructed workshop exit becomes a usable transfer point from which scenery can proceed along two distinct courses.
- **Sufficiency:** The participants release the door, pass a full-sized item through and verify both onward directions. Finding an unlocked door would not by itself demonstrate this practical opening.
- **Persistence:** The door can be set back again to enable repeated movement to the rehearsal stage or loading bay.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 10, *Source field*. Cell 10 would be achieved by identifying an available exit. Here the completed achievement is successful passage through the focal bottleneck with usable branching continuations.
- **Decisive evidence:** “Straight through,” followed by the confirmed left and right movements for different pieces of scenery.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

**Cell 12 documentary count:** 43 numbered examples; 3 structural signatures; 4 extended demonstrations.

## Cell 13 — Alternate passage

**Canonical identity:** Opening × Distributed × Standing

**Coordinate-generated form:** Distributed passage standing

**Original X–Y traversal:** X reaches Y by different path Z.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** A restricted repertoire becomes a definite range of available alternatives or passages. The completion is the expanded field of options itself.

**Canonical short form:** Another way through.

### Collected sentences and examples

1. The new harbour chart showed three different routes to the island.
2. A newly drawn map revealed four walkable ways around the landslide.
3. The station opened three new exits onto different streets.
4. With the canal bridge closed, two other crossings remained available.
5. We don't have to buy the equipment outright; we can hire it, borrow it or share the cost with another group.
6. There are several ways into this conversation: through what happened, how you felt or what you hoped would happen.
7. Instead of replacing the entire floor, we could sand it, cover it or restore the damaged sections.
8. You can learn the song by ear, from the sheet music or by watching someone play it.
9. We could raise the money through a sale, a sponsored walk or a concert.
10. There are now three ways to heat the house: the stove, the heat pump and the electric radiators.
11. **Nature:** A braided river develops multiple channels around shifting sediment bars
12. **Mathematics:** A graph gains alternative routes between previously disconnected vertices.
13. **Music:** A melodic cell develops two distinct viable continuations at a fork.
14. **Visual art:** Several paths through a sculpture garden become visible at once.
15. **Narrative:** The plot opens three credible futures for a protagonist.
16. **Architecture:** Several alternate public routes through a site become available.
17. **Social:** A community develops several workable routes for participation.
18. **Perception:** Several perceptual interpretations become genuinely available.
19. **Computation:** A service exposes multiple independent API routes.
20. The mountain does not move, but every climber discovers a different approach.
21. An open door can mean welcome, escape, opportunity, or a forgotten obligation.
22. A dictionary records words, but conversation keeps finding uses no definition anticipated.
23. A catalyst speeds a reaction by providing a pathway with a lower activation energy.
24. Several routes to the station are now known and available, not just one.
25. A river divides into braided channels.
26. More than one connected route becomes available.
27. A delayed journey gains three feasible routes after a timetable change.
28. Three clearly separate mint routes across a peach plane: multiplicity itself is the result.
29. Another way is a real change even before anyone takes it.
30. A revised timetable establishes several feasible routes home instead of a single narrow connection. A branching crack opens multiple passages through a material. Their availability does not depend on subsequent observation.
31. A writer finds several workable endings for a story that had seemed to admit only one.
32. A branching crack opens multiple passages through a material. Their availability does not depend on subsequent observation.
33. A revised timetable establishes several feasible routes home instead of a single narrow connection.
34. The connected side lanes now provide another complete route around the closed main road.
35. The argument reaches its conclusion by a second established chain of steps.
36. A maze reveals several traversable routes.
37. A maze with several independently lit crossings.
38. The range of available paths is genuinely wider now.

### Structural signatures

- A restricted field opens into alternatives.
- The available passages are established across the relevant range.
- As a definite repertoire of options.

### Extended demonstration — Dialogue cycle 1

*Two friends are planning how to reach a concert across town after discovering their usual bus has been cancelled.*

**Nora:** That's the last direct bus gone. I suppose we'll have to get a taxi.

**Ibrahim:** Maybe. Is the concert hall anywhere near the green tram line?

**Nora:** It is, but we're on the other side of the river.

**Ibrahim:** We can cross at the pedestrian bridge and catch the tram at Market Square.

**Nora:** That's one way. What if the tram's crowded?

**Ibrahim:** There should be a second bus from the station. It goes around the north side and stops behind the hall.

**Nora:** Does it run this late?

**Ibrahim:** Let me check. Yes, every twenty minutes until eleven.

**Nora:** We could also walk from here. The map says thirty-five minutes.

**Ibrahim:** We'd have to leave now, but we'd still get there before the support act.

**Nora:** So the cancelled bus doesn't leave us stranded. We've got the tram by the bridge, the station bus, or the walk.

**Ibrahim:** The tram's probably easiest. But if it's delayed, we know the other two are there.

**Nora:** Good. I'll stop looking at taxi prices.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 1

- **Change:** A trip initially experienced as blocked by one cancelled service becomes a determinate range of available passages.
- **Sufficiency:** Multiple independently available routes across the relevant transport network are established, each capable of reaching the concert hall; the opening is not exhausted by one privileged access point.
- **Persistence:** The alternative routes stand as an available repertoire from which the travellers may choose as conditions change.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 11, *Source-carried divergence*. No single entitlement or enabling connection constitutes these alternatives. Their availability is established across different routes in the network.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended demonstration — Dialogue cycle 2

*Two residents are planning how people can reach the hall during roadworks.*

**Rosie:** The road outside the main gate will be closed on Saturday. Won't that keep visitors away?

**Khalid:** Not necessarily. Pedestrians can still come along Church Lane.

**Rosie:** The notice says that lane stays open. What about the bus stop?

**Khalid:** The bus is being diverted to Market Street. There's a footpath from there through the park.

**Rosie:** And drivers?

**Khalid:** The temporary car park behind the school is open, with a path to the hall's rear gate.

**Rosie:** Let's put those on the visitor notice: Church Lane on foot, the park path from the bus, and the school car park for drivers.

**Khalid:** Good. They're quite different ways in, but all three are available.

**Rosie:** So the closed road doesn't leave us with only one awkward workaround.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 2

- **Change:** Access apparently reduced to a blocked road becomes an established repertoire of alternative passages.
- **Sufficiency:** Availability is demonstrated across independent routes for walkers, bus users and drivers; no single privileged entrance or entitlement establishes the full range.
- **Persistence:** The alternative passages stand as a determinate set of routes for visitors.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 11, *Source-carried divergence*. These options do not originate from one constitutive access right; they are available across a distributed transport field.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended demonstration — Dialogue cycle 3

*Two friends are arranging how visitors will reach a village book fair while the bridge is closed.*

**Nell:** The main road is shut all weekend. Should we tell everyone to come by the footbridge?

**Harun:** That's one way, but it won't help people bringing boxes of books.

**Nell:** There's a road around the mill, isn't there?

**Harun:** Yes. The council notice says it's open to cars. And the bus is stopping by the school instead of the square.

**Nell:** Is there a walking route from the school?

**Harun:** Along the riverside path. It comes out behind the library.

**Nell:** So we have the footbridge for people walking from town, the mill road for drivers, and the school path for bus passengers.

**Harun:** Three approaches, none dependent on the closed bridge.

**Nell:** I'll put all three on the fair notice, with a warning that the riverside path is gravel.

**Harun:** Good. People can choose whichever approach suits their journey.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 3

- **Change:** The fair, apparently cut off by a bridge closure, acquires a determinate set of available approaches across the surrounding area.
- **Sufficiency:** The footbridge, mill road and school path are separately verified as usable approaches for different modes of arrival; the achievement is not concentrated in one entrance or permission.
- **Persistence:** The alternatives stand as a published repertoire of routes for visitors.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 11, *Source-carried divergence*. There is no single credential or focal source constituting the routes. Their availability is distributed across independent passages.
- **Decisive evidence:** “Three approaches, none dependent on the closed bridge.”

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

**Cell 13 documentary count:** 38 numbered examples; 3 structural signatures; 3 complete dialogues.

## Cell 14 — Span-carried reach

**Canonical identity:** Opening × Distributed × Bearing

**Coordinate-generated form:** Span-bearing reach

**Original X–Y traversal:** X affects distant Y.

**Canonical structural characterisation:** A field of possibilities becomes available through a determinate pattern of enabling conditions. The achieved latitude is differentiated by what supports access across that field.

**Canonical short form:** Reachable across.

### Collected sentences and examples

1. With stepping stones across every stream, the children could reach cottages throughout the valley.
2. A sequence of relay towers brought the emergency signal to every hamlet in the mountains.
3. Linked ramps made each floor of the museum accessible to wheelchair users.
4. A network of ferries connected villages scattered across the islands.
5. Through relatives on both sides of the family, she now has access to memories spanning four generations.
6. The shared lending arrangements between our libraries give us access to collections throughout the county.
7. With family nearby for childcare, colleagues willing to cover shifts and a tutor available online, she has room to pursue her studies around her working life.
8. Through partnerships with local workshops, studios and theatres, the art students have access to a wide range of creative facilities.
9. The pension, savings and occasional freelance work give her access to different ways of spending her retirement.
10. The network of mentors gives the apprentices access to expertise at every level of the trade.
11. **Nature:** A fungal hypha spans nutrient-poor soil to reach a distant patch
12. **Mathematics:** A feasible region is described by inequalities whose conditions determine membership.
13. **Music:** A modulation passage affords several melodic continuations, each under different accompanying chords.
14. **Visual art:** Mirrors open sightlines across rooms under particular angles.
15. **Narrative:** Different characters gain access to separate truths through distinct confidants.
16. **Architecture:** Access across floors depends on a functioning lift-and-ramp network.
17. **Social:** Different members participate through their own enabling supports.
18. **Perception:** Attention can access different targets through distinct cue conditions.
19. **Computation:** Different routes are available under their respective authorization scopes.
20. Visual signals from the retina travel toward the brain through the optic nerve.
21. A chain of connections makes a distant village reachable. Access holds through the whole span.
22. A fungal strand reaches distant nutrients.
23. A living connection carries access across the soil.
24. A career adviser lists apprenticeships alongside the funding and prerequisites that make each reachable.
25. Saffron bridges tied to individual cobalt anchor points across a wide span: each reach has its support.
26. Access has a structure of support.
27. A travel pass opens different parts of a network at different times, with access constituted by the pass’s coverage. Several channels in a membrane become available under different physical gating conditions. The conditional access field exists independently of a verbal account.
28. A grammar makes several expressions available depending on tense, person, and register; the conditions establish a usable field of alternatives.
29. Several channels in a membrane become available under different physical gating conditions. The conditional access field exists independently of a verbal account.
30. A travel pass opens different parts of a network at different times, with access constituted by the pass’s coverage.
31. The far bank is reachable through the support of the bridge across the intervening span.
32. The upstairs room is accessible through the continuous flight of stairs connecting the levels.
33. Stepping stones connect opposite riverbanks.
34. A bridge of small suspended links spanning an open gap.
35. Reach comes from the links between one point and the next.

### Structural signatures

- A span of possibilities becomes accessible.
- Its enabling connections support access across the field.
- As reach borne by those enabling conditions.

### Extended documentary example — Cycle 1

*At a community sports centre, two parents are trying to work out which activities their family membership covers.*

**Helen:** I thought the family pass meant we could use everything here.

**Marco:** I thought so too, but the climbing wall page says there's an age rule.

**Helen:** Our youngest is eight. Does that rule her out?

**Marco:** Not necessarily. It says children eight to eleven can climb with an adult in the beginner session.

**Helen:** And swimming?

**Marco:** Family members can use the pool during public swim hours. The early morning lane session is adults only.

**Helen:** What about badminton? I'd booked that for Saturday.

**Marco:** Courts are included, but only when you've reserved a slot. Membership doesn't automatically reserve one.

**Helen:** So it's not one blanket yes or no.

**Marco:** Right. Let me write it down: pool during public hours, climbing in the supervised beginner session for her age, and badminton with a court booking.

**Helen:** And all of those are covered by the family pass once we meet the relevant conditions?

**Marco:** That's how the membership page puts it. We don't need to buy separate admission for them.

**Helen:** Good. I can see what we can actually arrange for the weekend now.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 1

- **Change:** A vague possibility of centre-wide access becomes a determinate spread of accessible activities under their respective qualifying conditions.
- **Sufficiency:** Reach is established across swimming, climbing, and badminton only through the eligibility rules and bookings constitutive of each opening; neither unrestricted access nor an isolated permission would describe it.
- **Persistence:** The accessible range remains constituted by the relation of membership, age, supervision, session times, and reservations to each activity.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. There the range of alternatives stands as an established set of available routes. Here the achieved range is inseparable from the conditions under which each possibility is available.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended documentary example — Cycle 2

*Two organisers are checking which hall rooms different community groups may book.*

**Freya:** I told the pottery group they could use any room, but the booking rules look more complicated.

**Dessie:** The small kitchen is only available with someone who's completed the food-safety induction.

**Freya:** The activity room doesn't need that. What about the upstairs room?

**Dessie:** It can be booked by groups with an access plan for anyone who can't use the stairs.

**Freya:** And the stage?

**Dessie:** That's available when the trained lighting volunteer is present.

**Freya:** So it isn't a simple yes-or-no permission for the whole building.

**Dessie:** No. Each space is reachable under a different condition. Pottery can book the activity room now, use the kitchen after induction, and use the stage with a qualified helper.

**Freya:** I'll amend the form so people can see what qualifies them for each room.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 2

- **Change:** Vague building-wide permission becomes a determinate spread of possible bookings under distinct qualifying conditions.
- **Sufficiency:** Access is established across the available rooms specifically through their respective eligibility, safety and support conditions.
- **Persistence:** The openings remain constituted by the relation between each room and its qualifying requirements.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. That form establishes a repertoire of passages; this form's reach exists only through the conditions that constitute each option.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

### Extended documentary example — Cycle 3

*Two coordinators are checking which parts of a municipal arts centre a youth group may use.*

**Ivo:** The group leader asked whether their booking gives them the whole building.

**Claudia:** It doesn't. They can use the rehearsal room, but the recording booth needs a technician present.

**Ivo:** And the print studio?

**Claudia:** Only after the safety induction. They've completed that, according to the register.

**Ivo:** The gallery is open to the public during the afternoon, so they can visit it anyway.

**Claudia:** Yes, but visiting isn't the same as booking it for their own exhibition.

**Ivo:** What about the roof terrace? Their leader mentioned a photography session there.

**Claudia:** It needs a staff escort. The rota has someone free at eleven on Friday; I've reserved that hour.

**Ivo:** Can we also arrange the technician for the recording booth?

**Claudia:** Yes, from two until four. I've booked that as well. And the gallery is for visiting, not putting up their own exhibition.

**Ivo:** So they have the rehearsal room, the print studio after their completed induction, the booth with a technician, and the terrace with an escort. They can visit the gallery too.

**Claudia:** Right. I'll send the confirmed schedule and put the condition beside each space.

**Ivo:** Good. Now the leader knows the full range of places they can use and what each one requires.

#### Archive structural check — Cycle 3

- **Change:** An assumed whole-building permission becomes a definite range of accessible spaces under distinct conditions.
- **Sufficiency:** The induction, technician and escort are accounted for, and visiting the gallery is distinguished from exhibition use.
- **Persistence:** The confirmed schedule carries the range and its respective qualifications.
- **Closest rival cell and decisive difference:** Cell 13, *Alternate passage*. The result depends on conditions attaching to each opening, not a free-standing set of alternatives.
- **Decisive evidence:** The complete schedule records the confirmed access and qualifications for every space discussed.

**Assessment:** Target achieved.

**Dialogue archive coverage:** 3 of 3 complete dialogues; 3 original structural checks.

**Cell 14 documentary count:** 35 numbered examples; 3 structural signatures; 3 complete dialogues.
